Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Given the integer $n$ — the number of available blocks. You must use all blocks to build a pedestal.

The pedestal consists of $3$ platforms for $2$-nd, $1$-st and $3$-rd places respectively. The platform for the $1$-st place must be strictly higher than for the $2$-nd place, and the platform for the $2$-nd place must be strictly higher than for the $3$-rd place. Also, the height of each platform must be greater than zero (that is, each platform must contain at least one block).

Example pedestal of $n=11$ blocks: second place height equals $4$ blocks, first place height equals $5$ blocks, third place height equals $2$ blocks.

Among all possible pedestals of $n$ blocks, deduce one such that the platform height for the $1$-st place minimum as possible. If there are several of them, output any of them.


-----Input-----

The first line of input data contains an integer $t$ ($1 \le t \le 10^4$) — the number of test cases.

Each test case contains a single integer $n$ ($6 \le n \le 10^5$) — the total number of blocks for the pedestal. All $n$ blocks must be used.

It is guaranteed that the sum of $n$ values over all test cases does not exceed $10^6$.


-----Output-----

For each test case, output $3$ numbers $h_2, h_1, h_3$ — the platform heights for $2$-nd, $1$-st and $3$-rd places on a pedestal consisting of $n$ blocks ($h_1+h_2+h_3=n$, $0 < h_3 < h_2 < h_1$).

Among all possible pedestals, output the one for which the value of $h_1$ minimal. If there are several of them, output any of them.


-----Examples-----

Input
6
11
6
10
100000
7
8
Output
4 5 2
2 3 1
4 5 1
33334 33335 33331
2 4 1
3 4 1


-----Note-----

In the first test case we can not get the height of the platform for the first place less than $5$, because if the height of the platform for the first place is not more than $4$, then we can use at most $4 + 3 + 2 = 9$ blocks. And we should use $11 = 4 + 5 + 2$ blocks. Therefore, the answer 4 5 2 fits.

In the second set, the only suitable answer is: 2 3 1.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout


Now solve the problem and return the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to build a pedestal with 3 platforms for 2nd, 1st, and 3rd places
2. Constraints: `h_3 < h_2 < h_1` (strictly increasing) and all heights > 0
3. Total blocks: `h_1 + h_2 + h_3 = n`
4. We want to minimize `h_1` (height of 1st place platform)
5. If multiple solutions have the same minimal `h_1`, any is acceptable

## Analysis

To minimize `h_1`, we need to maximize the sum of `h_2 + h_3` while keeping `h_3 < h_2 < h_1`.

The constraint `h_3 < h_2 < h_1` means:
- `h_3 ≥ 1`
- `h_2 ≥ h_3 + 1` 
- `h_1 ≥ h_2 + 1`

So the minimum possible values are:
- `h_3 = 1` 
- `h_2 = 2` (since it must be > h_3)
- `h_1 = 3` (since it must be > h_2)

But we need to check if this works with the total sum.

For a given `h_1`, the minimum possible sum of `h_2 + h_3` is:
- `h_3 ≥ 1`
- `h_2 ≥ 2` 
- `h_3 < h_2 < h_1`

So minimum `h_2 + h_3 = 1 + 2 = 3` when `h_1 > 2`.

Therefore, we need: `h_1 + (h_2 + h_3) = n`, so `h_1 + (at least 3) = n`, which means `h_1 ≥ n - (h_2 + h_3)`.

To minimize `h_1`, we want to maximize `h_2 + h_3` given the constraints.

So `h_1 + h_2 + h_3 ≥ (x+2) + (x+1) + x = 3x + 3`.

We have `h_1 + h_2 + h_3 = n`, so `n ≥ 3x + 3`, which means `x ≥ (n-3)/3`.

But we also need `h_1` to be as small as possible.

Actually, let's try to build from the minimum values:

For minimal `h_1`, we want to maximize the sum of `h_2 + h_3` given that `h_2 < h_1` and `h_3 < h_2`.

To maximize `h_2 + h_3` given `h_2 < h_1`, we should set `h_2 = h_1 - 1` and `h_3 = h_1 - 2` (as long as `h_1 > 2`).

But we need `h_2 + h_3 = n - h_1`, so:
- `(h_1 - 1) + (h_1 - 2) = 2h_1 - 3 = n - h_1`
- `3h_1 = n + 3`
- `h_1 = (n + 3) / 3`

This works when `(n + 3)` is divisible by 3.

If not divisible, we need to adjust.

2. Check if `h_1 + h_2 + h_3 = n`
3. If not, adjust by reducing `h_1` and distributing the difference

## Problem Understanding

We need to build a pedestal with 3 platforms (2nd, 1st, 3rd places) such that:
- Total blocks used = n
- Each platform has height > 0
- h₁ > h₂ > h₃ (1st place strictly higher than 2nd, 2nd strictly higher than 3rd)
- We want to minimize h₁ (the height of the 1st place platform)

## Key Insights

1. We need to minimize h₁ while satisfying h₁ > h₂ > h₃ and h₁ + h₂ + h₃ = n
2. To minimize h₁, we want h₂ and h₃ to be as large as possible while still satisfying h₂ < h₁ and h₃ < h₂
3. The optimal configuration would have h₂ = h₁ - 1 and h₃ = h₂ - 1 = h₁ - 2
4. This gives us: h₁ + (h₁ - 1) + (h₁ - 2) = 3h₁ - 3 = n
5. So: 3h₁ = n + 3, and h₁ = (n + 3) / 3

## Algorithm

1. h₁ = (n + 5) // 3
2. h₂ = h₁ - 1  
3. h₃ = h₁ - 2
4. deficit = (3 * h₁ - 3) - n  # this is the excess over n
5. h₃ = h₃ - deficit

We need to ensure h₃ > 0. Since h₃ starts at h₁ - 2 and we're subtracting deficit, we need to verify.

Actually, from the constraint h₁ ≥ (n + 3) / 3, we have 3h₁ - 3 ≥ n, so deficit ≥ 0.

And h₃ = h₁ - 2 - deficit ≥ 1?

We need to ensure h₃ ≥ 1.

Let me check the minimum h₃:

h₃ = h₁ - 2 - deficit = h₁ - 2 - ((3h₁ - 3) - n) = h₁ - 2 - 3h₁ + 3 + n = -2h₁ + 1 + n

We need -2h₁ + 1 + n ≥ 1, so n - 2h₁ + 1 ≥ 1, so n ≥ 2h₁.

But h₁ = (n + 5) // 3, so is n ≥ 2*(n + 5) // 3?

For large n, 2n/3 ≈ 2n/3, so n ≥ 2n/3 is true.

Let me check with n=6: h₁=3, h₃ = -2*3 + 1 + 6 = -6 + 1 + 6 = 1 ≥ 1 ✓
n=10: h₁=5, h₃ = -2*5 + 1 + 10 = -10 + 1 + 10 = 1 ≥ 1 ✓
n=11: h₁=5, h₃ = -2*5 + 1 + 11 = -10 + 1 + 11 = 2 ≥ 1 ✓

Perfect!

## Final Solution

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    
    # Minimum h1 such that we can have h2 = h1-1, h3 = h1-2
    h1 = (n + 5) // 3
    h2 = h1 - 1
    h3 = h1 - 2
    
    # Calculate excess blocks
    excess = (3 * h1 - 3) - n
    
    # Reduce h3 by the excess
    h3 -= excess
    
    print(h2, h1, h3)
```

- sum = 4 + 3 + 2 = 9 > 7, excess = 2
- h3 = 2 - 2 = 0 ❌ (h3 must be > 0)

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    h1 = (n + 2) // 3 + 1
    h3 = max(1, n - 2 * h1 + 1)
    h2 = n - h1 - h3
    print(h2, h1, h3)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Given the integer $n$ — the number of available blocks. You must use all blocks to build a pedestal.

The pedestal consists of $3$ platforms for $2$-nd, $1$-st and $3$-rd places respectively. The platform for the $1$-st place must be strictly higher than for the $2$-nd place, and the platform for the $2$-nd place must be strictly higher than for the $3$-rd place. Also, the height of each platform must be greater than zero (that is, each platform must contain at least one block).

Example pedestal of $n=11$ blocks: second place height equals $4$ blocks, first place height equals $5$ blocks, third place height equals $2$ blocks.

Among all possible pedestals of $n$ blocks, deduce one such that the platform height for the $1$-st place minimum as possible. If there are several of them, output any of them.


-----Input-----

The first line of input data contains an integer $t$ ($1 \le t \le 10^4$) — the number of test cases.

Each test case contains a single integer $n$ ($6 \le n \le 10^5$) — the total number of blocks for the pedestal. All $n$ blocks must be used.

It is guaranteed that the sum of $n$ values over all test cases does not exceed $10^6$.


-----Output-----

For each test case, output $3$ numbers $h_2, h_1, h_3$ — the platform heights for $2$-nd, $1$-st and $3$-rd places on a pedestal consisting of $n$ blocks ($h_1+h_2+h_3=n$, $0 < h_3 < h_2 < h_1$).

Among all possible pedestals, output the one for which the value of $h_1$ minimal. If there are several of them, output any of them.


-----Examples-----

Input
6
11
6
10
100000
7
8
Output
4 5 2
2 3 1
4 5 1
33334 33335 33331
2 4 1
3 4 1


-----Note-----

In the first test case we can not get the height of the platform for the first place less than $5$, because if the height of the platform for the first place is not more than $4$, then we can use at most $4 + 3 + 2 = 9$ blocks. And we should use $11 = 4 + 5 + 2$ blocks. Therefore, the answer 4 5 2 fits.

In the second set, the only suitable answer is: 2 3 1.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to build a pedestal with 3 platforms for 2nd, 1st, and 3rd places
2. Constraints: `h1 > h2 > h3 > 0` and `h1 + h2 + h3 = n`
3. Goal: Minimize `h1` (height of 1st place platform)
4. If multiple solutions exist with minimal `h1`, output any

## Key Insight

To minimize `h1`, we need to maximize the sum of `h2 + h3` while maintaining `h1 > h2 > h3 > 0`.

The optimal strategy is to make `h2` and `h3` as large as possible while still satisfying the constraints.

## Mathematical Approach

Let's think about the constraints:
- `h1 > h2 > h3 > 0`
- `h1 + h2 + h3 = n`

To minimize `h1`, we want to make `h2` and `h3` as large as possible, but with `h2 > h3`.

The optimal configuration would be something like:
- `h3 = x`
- `h2 = x + 1` 
- `h1 = n - h2 - h3 = n - (x + 1) - x = n - 2x - 1`

Constraints:
1. `h1 > h2` → `n - 2x - 1 > x + 1` → `n - 1 > 3x + 1` → `n - 2 > 3x` → `x < (n - 2) / 3`
2. `h3 > 0` → `x > 0`
3. All values must be positive integers

So `x` can be at most `floor((n - 3) / 3)` to satisfy the strict inequalities.

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    h1 = (n + 2) // 3 + 1
    if n - 2 * h1 + 1 >= 1:
        h2 = h1 - 1
        h3 = n - h1 - h2
    else:
        h2 = h1 - 2
        h3 = n - h1 - h2
    print(h2, h1, h3)
```

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    h1 = (n + 2) // 3 + 1
    if n - 2 * h1 + 1 > 0:
        h2 = h1 - 1
        h3 = n - h1 - h2
    else:
        h2 = h1 - 2
        h3 = n - h1 - h2
    print(h2, h1, h3)
```

But n - 2*h1 + 1 > 0 is equivalent to >= 1 since integers.

Actually, since we're dealing with integers, n - 2*h1 + 1 >= 1 is equivalent to n - 2*h1 + 1 > 0.

Yes.

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Given the integer $n$ — the number of available blocks. You must use all blocks to build a pedestal.

The pedestal consists of $3$ platforms for $2$-nd, $1$-st and $3$-rd places respectively. The platform for the $1$-st place must be strictly higher than for the $2$-nd place, and the platform for the $2$-nd place must be strictly higher than for the $3$-rd place. Also, the height of each platform must be greater than zero (that is, each platform must contain at least one block).

Example pedestal of $n=11$ blocks: second place height equals $4$ blocks, first place height equals $5$ blocks, third place height equals $2$ blocks.

Among all possible pedestals of $n$ blocks, deduce one such that the platform height for the $1$-st place minimum as possible. If there are several of them, output any of them.


-----Input-----

The first line of input data contains an integer $t$ ($1 \le t \le 10^4$) — the number of test cases.

Each test case contains a single integer $n$ ($6 \le n \le 10^5$) — the total number of blocks for the pedestal. All $n$ blocks must be used.

It is guaranteed that the sum of $n$ values over all test cases does not exceed $10^6$.


-----Output-----

For each test case, output $3$ numbers $h_2, h_1, h_3$ — the platform heights for $2$-nd, $1$-st and $3$-rd places on a pedestal consisting of $n$ blocks ($h_1+h_2+h_3=n$, $0 < h_3 < h_2 < h_1$).

Among all possible pedestals, output the one for which the value of $h_1$ minimal. If there are several of them, output any of them.


-----Examples-----

Input
6
11
6
10
100000
7
8
Output
4 5 2
2 3 1
4 5 1
33334 33335 33331
2 4 1
3 4 1


-----Note-----

In the first test case we can not get the height of the platform for the first place less than $5$, because if the height of the platform for the first place is not more than $4$, then we can use at most $4 + 3 + 2 = 9$ blocks. And we should use $11 = 4 + 5 + 2$ blocks. Therefore, the answer 4 5 2 fits.

In the second set, the only suitable answer is: 2 3 1.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout


Now solve the problem and return the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). ## Algorithm:

1. For each test case with n blocks:
   - h₁ = (n + 5) // 3  # This gives ceil((n + 3) / 3)
   - h₂ = min(h₁ - 1, n - h₁ - 1)  # Maximum possible value for h₂ that is less than h₁ and leaves at least 1 block for h₃
   - h₃ = (n - h₁) - h₂  # The remaining blocks

But wait, is h₂ = min(h₁ - 1, n - h₁ - 1) always optimal?
- h₂ < h₁ ⇒ h₂ ≤ h₁ - 1
- h₃ > 0 ⇒ h₃ ≥ 1 ⇒ h₂ ≤ n - h₁ - 1

So yes, h₂ ≤ min(h₁ - 1, n - h₁ - 1)

And since we're minimizing h₁ first, this gives us the minimal possible h₁ with valid heights.

## Verification:

n = 11:
h₁ = (11 + 5) // 3 = 16 // 3 = 5
h₂ = min(4, 11 - 5 - 1) = min(4, 5) = 4
h₃ = 11 - 5 - 4 = 2
Result: (4, 5, 2) ✓

n = 6:
h₁ = (6 + 5) // 3 = 11 // 3 = 3
h₂ = min(2, 6 - 3 - 1) = min(2, 2) = 2
h₃ = 6 - 3 - 2 = 1
Result: (2, 3, 1) ✓

n = 10:
h₁ = (10 + 5) // 3 = 15 // 3 = 5
h₂ = min(4, 10 - 5 - 1) = min(4, 4) = 4
h₃ = 10 - 5 - 4 = 1
Result: (4, 5, 1) ✓

All match!

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    h1 = (n + 5) // 3
    h2 = min(h1 - 1, n - h1 - 1)
    h3 = n - h1 - h2
    print(h2, h1, h3)
```

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to build a pedestal with 3 platforms for 2nd, 1st, and 3rd places
2. Constraints: h₁ > h₂ > h₃ > 0 (strictly decreasing)
3. Total blocks: h₁ + h₂ + h₃ = n
4. We want to minimize h₁ (the height of the 1st place platform)
5. We need to use all blocks

## Key Insight

To minimize h₁, we need to maximize the sum of h₂ and h₃ while maintaining the constraint h₁ > h₂ > h₃.

Since h₁ > h₂ > h₃, we can express:
- h₂ = h₃ + a (where a ≥ 1)
- h₁ = h₂ + b = h₃ + a + b (where b ≥ 1)

So total blocks: h₁ + h₂ + h₃ = (h₃ + a + b) + (h₃ + a) + h₃ = 3h₃ + 2a + b = n

To minimize h₁ = h₃ + a + b, we want to minimize this expression.

## Optimal Strategy

To minimize h₁, we should:
1. Make h₃ as large as possible (since it's the smallest)
2. Make the differences a and b as small as possible (minimum difference of 1 between adjacent platforms)

Let's assume the minimum differences:
- h₂ = h₃ + 1
- h₁ = h₂ + 1 = h₃ + 2

Then total blocks: h₁ + h₂ + h₃ = (h₃ + 2) + (h₃ + 1) + h₃ = 3h₃ + 3 = n
So h₃ = (n - 3) / 3
h₂ = (n - 3) / 3 + 1 = (n - 3 + 3) / 3 = n / 3
h₁ = (n - 3) / 3 + 2 = (n - 3 + 6) / 3 = (n + 3) / 3

So x = (n - 3) / 3

Now, once we have h₁, we can find h₂ and h₃.

We want to maximize h₂ + h₃ = n - h₁, subject to h₂ < h₁ and h₃ < h₂ and h₂ > h₃ > 0.

To maximize h₂ and h₃, we set h₂ = h₁ - 1 and h₃ = h₂ - 1 = h₁ - 2, but only if this sum is ≤ n - h₁.

## Final Algorithm

For each test case:
1. Read n
2. h₁ = (n + 5) // 3
3. remainder = n - h₁
4. h₂ = remainder // 2 + 1
5. h₃ = remainder - h₂
6. Output h₂, h₁, h₃

Wait, in the problem output order: "h_2, h_1, h_3"

So output: h₂, h₁, h₃

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    h1 = (n + 5) // 3
    remainder = n - h1
    h2 = remainder // 2 + 1
    h3 = remainder - h2
    print(h2, h1, h3)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Given the integer $n$ — the number of available blocks. You must use all blocks to build a pedestal.

The pedestal consists of $3$ platforms for $2$-nd, $1$-st and $3$-rd places respectively. The platform for the $1$-st place must be strictly higher than for the $2$-nd place, and the platform for the $2$-nd place must be strictly higher than for the $3$-rd place. Also, the height of each platform must be greater than zero (that is, each platform must contain at least one block).

Example pedestal of $n=11$ blocks: second place height equals $4$ blocks, first place height equals $5$ blocks, third place height equals $2$ blocks.

Among all possible pedestals of $n$ blocks, deduce one such that the platform height for the $1$-st place minimum as possible. If there are several of them, output any of them.


-----Input-----

The first line of input data contains an integer $t$ ($1 \le t \le 10^4$) — the number of test cases.

Each test case contains a single integer $n$ ($6 \le n \le 10^5$) — the total number of blocks for the pedestal. All $n$ blocks must be used.

It is guaranteed that the sum of $n$ values over all test cases does not exceed $10^6$.


-----Output-----

For each test case, output $3$ numbers $h_2, h_1, h_3$ — the platform heights for $2$-nd, $1$-st and $3$-rd places on a pedestal consisting of $n$ blocks ($h_1+h_2+h_3=n$, $0 < h_3 < h_2 < h_1$).

Among all possible pedestals, output the one for which the value of $h_1$ minimal. If there are several of them, output any of them.


-----Examples-----

Input
6
11
6
10
100000
7
8
Output
4 5 2
2 3 1
4 5 1
33334 33335 33331
2 4 1
3 4 1


-----Note-----

In the first test case we can not get the height of the platform for the first place less than $5$, because if the height of the platform for the first place is not more than $4$, then we can use at most $4 + 3 + 2 = 9$ blocks. And we should use $11 = 4 + 5 + 2$ blocks. Therefore, the answer 4 5 2 fits.

In the second set, the only suitable answer is: 2 3 1.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout


Now solve the problem and return the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.

```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

1. h₁ = (n + 2) // 3 + 1  # ceil((n+3)/3) = (n+2)//3 + 1
   Actually, ceil(a/b) = (a + b - 1) // b

   So ceil((n+3)/3) = (n+3 + 3 - 1) // 3 = (n+5)//3

   Check: n=6: (6+5)//3 = 11//3 = 3 ✓

   Yes! So h₁ = (n + 5) // 3

2. remaining = n - h₁
3. We need to split remaining into h₂ and h₃ with h₂ > h₃ ≥ 1 and h₂ < h₁.

4. If we can set h₃ = 1 and h₂ = remaining - 1, then use it (if h₂ < h₁ and h₂ > 1)
   - This requires remaining - 1 < h₁ and remaining - 1 > 1
   - i.e., remaining < h₁ + 1 and remaining > 2

5. If not, set h₃ = 2, h₂ = remaining - 2 (if h₂ > 2 and h₂ < h₁)

6. Since a solution exists, we can try increasing h₃ until we find one.

But we can be smarter. Since we only need one solution, and the minimal h₁, we can do:

- h₃ = 1
- h₂ = remaining - 1
- if h₂ < h₁ and h₂ > 1:
   - use this
- else:
   - h₃ = 2
   - h₂ = remaining - 2
   - if h₂ < h₁ and h₂ > 2:
   - use this
   - else: h₃ = 3, etc.

But since the total remaining is at most n - h₁, and h₁ is at least (n+3)/3, we have remaining = n - h₁ ≤ n - (n+3)/3 = (2n - 3)/3

And we know a valid split exists, so we can simply set h₃ = 1 and h₂ = remaining - 1, and if it doesn't work, try h₃ = 2.

For n=9: h₁ = (9+5)//3 = 14//3 = 4

So algorithm:
1. h1 = (n + 5) // 3
2. remaining = n - h1
3. for h3 in range(1, remaining):  # try from 1 upward
4.     h2 = remaining - h3
5.     if h2 > h3 and h2 < h1:
6.         break
7. output: h2, h1, h3

But we can do without loop since we know the pattern.

From examples:
- n=6: h1=3, remaining=3, h3=1, h2=2 → output 2,3,1 ✓
- n=7: h1=4, remaining=3, h3=1, h2=2 → output 2,4,1 ✓

- n=9: h1=4, remaining=5, h3=2, h2=3 → output 3,4,2

But expected for n=100000: 33334 33335 33331

Let me verify: h1 = (100000+5)//3 = 100005//3 = 33335
remaining = 100000 - 33335 = 66665
h3 = 33331, h2 = 66665 - 33331 = 33334
Check: h2 = 33334, h1 = 33335, h3 = 33331

So h3 = 33331, h2 = 33334.

How to compute this?

Notice that h2 is close to remaining/2, and h3 is close to that.

In the solution: h3 = 33331, h2 = 33334.

We can set h3 = remaining - h2, and we want a solution.

Since we only need any valid solution, and the minimal h1, we can set:

- h3 = 1
- h2 = remaining - 1
- if h2 < h1 and h2 > 1: use it
- else: h3 = 2, h2 = remaining - 2, etc.

But to avoid loop, we can do:

Since the only constraint is h2 > h3 and h2 < h1, and h3 = something, we can try to set h3 = min(1, something) but we know from the example that h3 is about remaining/2 - something.

Actually, in the example n=100000, remaining = 66665, h3 = 33331, h2 = 33334.

33331 + 33334 = 66665.

And 33331 is roughly half of 66665.

In general, if we set h3 = remaining // 2, then h2 = remaining - h3 = remaining - remaining//2 = remaining - (remaining//2)

If remaining is even: h3 = remaining/2, h2 = remaining/2, so h2 = h3 → not strictly greater.

If remaining is odd: h3 = remaining//2, h2 = remaining - remaining//2 = (remaining+1)//2 > remaining//2 ✓

So for odd remaining, set h3 = remaining//2, h2 = (remaining+1)//2

For even remaining, we need to adjust.

For even remaining, if we set h3 = remaining//2 - 1, then h2 = remaining - (remaining//2 - 1) = remaining - remaining//2 + 1 = (remaining+2)//2

We need h2 > h3: (remaining+2)//2 > remaining//2 - 1

For even remaining = 2k: 
h3 = k-1, h2 = k+1
h2 > h3: k+1 > k-1 ✓
h2 < h1: k+1 < h1

Since h1 = (n+5)//3, and remaining = n - h1, we have h1 = n - remaining.

We need k+1 < n - remaining, where k = remaining//2.

For large remaining, this should hold.

For example n=100000, remaining=66665 (odd), so use h3 = 33332, h2 = 33333? 
But expected is 33334,33335,33331.

33332 + 33333 = 66665.

But the expected has h3=33331, h2=33334.

Any solution is acceptable.

To match the expected format, let's just use the simple method with the loop, or a fixed choice.

Given that the sum of n over test cases is at most 10^6, and we only need to try a few values of h3 (at most 10 or so), we can use a loop.

But we can do it without loop by choosing:

- if remaining is odd:
  - h3 = remaining // 2
  - h2 = remaining - h3 = (remaining + 1) // 2
- if remaining is even:
  - h3 = remaining // 2 - 1
  - h2 = remaining - h3 = remaining - (remaining//2 - 1) = (remaining + 2) // 2

Check if h2 < h1:

For remaining even: we need (remaining + 2) // 2 < h1 = n - remaining
(remaining + 2) // 2 < n - remaining
Since remaining = n - h1, we have:
(remaining + 2) // 2 < n - remaining
(remaining + 2) // 2 + remaining < n
(3*remaining + 2) // 2 < n

For large n, this should hold, but let's verify with a small even case.

For n=8: h1 = (8+5)//3 = 13//3 = 4, remaining = 4 (even)
h3 = 4//2 - 1 = 1, h2 = 4 - 1 = 3
3 < 4 ✓, 3 > 1 ✓

For n=6: remaining = 3 (odd), h3 = 1, h2 = 2 ✓

For n=7: remaining = 3 (odd), h3 = 1, h2 = 2 ✓

For n=9: remaining = 5 (odd), h3 = 2, h2 = 3 ✓

For n=10: remaining = 5 (odd), h3 = 2, h2 = 3
Sum = 3+2=5, total = 3+4+2 = 9, but n=10.

n=10: h1 = (10+5)//3 = 15//3 = 5, remaining = 5
h3 = 2, h2 = 3, sum=5, total=5+3+2=10 ✓

Perfect.

So the final algorithm:

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    h1 = (n + 5) // 3  # ceil((n+3)/3)
    remaining = n - h1
    
    if remaining % 2 == 1:
        h3 = remaining // 2
        h2 = remaining - h3
    else:
        h3 = remaining // 2 - 1
        h2 = remaining - h3
    
    # Verify constraints: h1 > h2 > h3 > 0
    # h2 > h3: should hold by construction
    # h2 < h1: should hold for given n
    print(h2, h1, h3)
```

Let me verify with the provided examples:

n=11: h1 = (11+5)//3 = 16//3 = 5, remaining = 6 (even)
h3 = 6//2 - 1 = 3-1 = 2, h2 = 6-2 = 4
Output: 4, 5, 2 ✓

n=6: h1 = (6+5)//3 = 11//3 = 3, remaining = 3 (odd)
h3 = 3//2 = 1, h2 = 3-1 = 2
Output: 2, 3, 1 ✓

n=10: h1 = (10+5)//3 = 15//3 = 5, remaining = 5 (odd)
h3 = 5//2 = 2, h2 = 5-2 = 3
Output: 3, 5, 2? But expected is 4,5,1.

The problem says "if there are several of them, output any of them."

So 3,5,2 is valid: 5>3>2 and sum=10 ✓.

But expected is 4,5,1.

We can output any valid one.

Similarly, for n=100000: 
h1 = (100000+5)//3 = 100005//3 = 33335
remaining = 100000 - 33335 = 66665 (odd)
h3 = 66665//2 = 33332, h2 = 66665 - 33332 = 33333
Output: 33333, 33335, 33332

But expected is 33334, 33335, 33331.

Both are valid.

So the algorithm is correct.

Final code:

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    h1 = (n + 5) // 3
    remaining = n - h1
    
    if remaining % 2 == 1:
        h3 = remaining // 2
        h2 = remaining - h3
    else:
        h3 = remaining // 2 - 1
        h2 = remaining - h3
    
    print(h2, h1, h3)
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Given the integer $n$ — the number of available blocks. You must use all blocks to build a pedestal.

The pedestal consists of $3$ platforms for $2$-nd, $1$-st and $3$-rd places respectively. The platform for the $1$-st place must be strictly higher than for the $2$-nd place, and the platform for the $2$-nd place must be strictly higher than for the $3$-rd place. Also, the height of each platform must be greater than zero (that is, each platform must contain at least one block).

Example pedestal of $n=11$ blocks: second place height equals $4$ blocks, first place height equals $5$ blocks, third place height equals $2$ blocks.

Among all possible pedestals of $n$ blocks, deduce one such that the platform height for the $1$-st place minimum as possible. If there are several of them, output any of them.


-----Input-----

The first line of input data contains an integer $t$ ($1 \le t \le 10^4$) — the number of test cases.

Each test case contains a single integer $n$ ($6 \le n \le 10^5$) — the total number of blocks for the pedestal. All $n$ blocks must be used.

It is guaranteed that the sum of $n$ values over all test cases does not exceed $10^6$.


-----Output-----

For each test case, output $3$ numbers $h_2, h_1, h_3$ — the platform heights for $2$-nd, $1$-st and $3$-rd places on a pedestal consisting of $n$ blocks ($h_1+h_2+h_3=n$, $0 < h_3 < h_2 < h_1$).

Among all possible pedestals, output the one for which the value of $h_1$ minimal. If there are several of them, output any of them.


-----Examples-----

Input

11
6
10
100000
7
8
Output

2 3 1
4 5 1
33334 33335 33331
2 4 1
3 4 1


-----Note-----

In the first test case we can not get the height of the platform for the first place less than $5$, because if the height of the platform for the first place is not more than $4$, then we can use at most $4 + 3 + 2 = 9$ blocks. And we should use $11 = 4 + 5 + 2$ blocks. Therefore, the answer 4 5 2 fits.

In the second set, the only suitable answer is: 2 3 1.

## Algorithm

1. For each test case with n blocks:

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    k = n // 3 + 2  # h1
    h2 = (n - k) // 2 + 1  # h2 > h3
    h3 = n - k - h2  # h3
    
    print(h2, k, h3)
```

Since k > (n+2)/3, and (n-k)/2 + 1 = (n-k+2)/2
We need k > (n-k+2)/2
2k > n-k+2
3k > n+2
k > (n+2)/3 ✓

Also need h3 ≥ 1:
h3 = n - k - h2 = n - k - (floor((n-k)/2) + 1) = (n-k-1)//2 - 1 + 1? Wait

Actually: h3 = n - k - h2 = n - k - (floor((n-k)/2) + 1) = (n-k) - floor((n-k)/2) - 1 = ceil((n-k)/2) - 1? No.

Let m = n-k
h3 = m - (floor(m/2) + 1) = m - floor(m/2) - 1 = ceil(m/2) - 1

We need h3 ≥ 1, so ceil(m/2) - 1 ≥ 1, so ceil(m/2) ≥ 2, so m ≥ 3.

So we need m ≥ 3, i.e., n-k ≥ 3.

With k = floor((n+2)/3) + 1, is n-k ≥ 3?

n-k = n - (floor((n+2)/3) + 1) = n - floor((n+2)/3) - 1

For large n: n - (n+2)/3 - 1 = (2n-8)/3 → positive for n > 4.

For n=6: 6 - floor(8/3) - 1 = 6 - 2 - 1 = 3 ≥ 3 ✓
For n=7: 7 - floor(9/3) - 1 = 7 - 3 - 1 = 3 ≥ 3 ✓
For n=8: 8 - floor(10/3) - 1 = 8 - 3 - 1 = 4 ≥ 3 ✓

So yes.

Therefore, the final algorithm:

```python
t = int(input())

    n = int(input())

    m = n - k
    h2 = m // 2 + 1  # greater than h3
    h3 = m - h2
    
    print(h2, k, h3)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Given the integer $n$ — the number of available blocks. You must use all blocks to build a pedestal.

The pedestal consists of $3$ platforms for $2$-nd, $1$-st and $3$-rd places respectively. The platform for the $1$-st place must be strictly higher than for the $2$-nd place, and the platform for the $2$-nd place must be strictly higher than for the $3$-rd place. Also, the height of each platform must be greater than zero (that is, each platform must contain at least one block).

Example pedestal of $n=11$ blocks: second place height equals $4$ blocks, first place height equals $5$ blocks, third place height equals $2$ blocks.

Among all possible pedestals of $n$ blocks, deduce one such that the platform height for the $1$-st place minimum as possible. If there are several of them, output any of them.


-----Input-----

The first line of input data contains an integer $t$ ($1 \le t \le 10^4$) — the number of test cases.

Each test case contains a single integer $n$ ($6 \le n \le 10^5$) — the total number of blocks for the pedestal. All $n$ blocks must be used.

It is guaranteed that the sum of $n$ values over all test cases does not exceed $10^6$.


-----Output-----

For each test case, output $3$ numbers $h_2, h_1, h_3$ — the platform heights for $2$-nd, $1$-st and $3$-rd places on a pedestal consisting of $n$ blocks ($h_1+h_2+h_3=n$, $0 < h_3 < h_2 < h_1$).

Among all possible pedestals, output the one for which the value of $h_1$ minimal. If there are several of them, output any of them.


-----Examples-----

Input
6
11
6
10
100000
7
8
Output
4 5 2
2 3 1
4 5 1
33334 33335 33331
2 4 1
3 4 1


-----Note-----

In the first test case we can not get the height of the platform for the first place less than $5$, because if the height of the platform for the first place is not more than $4$, then we can use at most $4 + 3 + 2 = 9$ blocks. And we should use $11 = 4 + 5 + 2$ blocks. Therefore, the answer 4 5 2 fits.

In the second set, the only suitable answer is: 2 3 1.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

```python
import math

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    h1 = (n + 3 + 2) // 3  # ceil((n+3)/3)
    h3 = n - 2 * h1 + 1
    if h3 > 0:
        h2 = h1 - 1
        print(h2, h1, h3)
    else:
        h3 = 1
        h2 = n - h1 - h3
        print(h2, h1, h3)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You have an n by m grid. Each grid square has a certain value associated with it. This is given by the numbers vi,j.

You can capture grid squares in two different ways.
You can directly capture a grid square. This costs ci,j.
 You can indirectly capture a grid square. You can only do this if we have already captured all of this squares neighbors. This costs bi,j ≤ ci,j.

Two squares are neighbors if they share an edge.

Your score is the sum of values you have captured minus the cost it took to capture those squares.
Return the maximum possible score you can achieve.

 Input format: 
The first line of input will contain two integers n, m.

The next n lines of input will contain m space separated integers each.
The j-th number on the i-th line denotes vi,j.

The next n lines of input will contain m space separated integers each.
The j-th number on the i-th line denotes bi,j.

The next n lines of input will contain m space separated integers each.
The j-th number on the i-th line denotes ci,j.

 Output format: 
Print a single integer, the answer to the problem.

 Constraints 
For all subtasks:
0 ≤ vi,j ≤ 100,000 
0 ≤ bi,j ≤ ci,j ≤ 100,000 
1 ≤ n, m

 Subtask 1 (45 pts): 
n, m ≤ 3

 Subtask 2 (35 pts): 
n, m ≤ 7

 Subtask 3 (20 pts): 
n, m ≤ 25

SAMPLE INPUT
3 5

9 0 3 2 4

0 2 8 6 2

5 3 4 1 3

5 1 7 9 5

2 4 1 2 4

9 1 2 2 0

9 1 8 9 7

2 7 3 2 9

9 1 9 8 2

SAMPLE OUTPUT
13

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m grid where each cell has:
   - A value `v[i][j]` (the value we gain when capturing)
   - A cost `b[i][j]` to capture indirectly (only if all neighbors are captured)
   - A cost `c[i][j]` to capture directly

2. We can capture a cell in two ways:
   - Directly: cost `c[i][j]`
   - Indirectly: cost `b[i][j]` (only if all neighbors are already captured)

3. Goal: Maximize the score = sum of values captured - sum of costs to capture

## Key Insights

1. If we capture a cell indirectly, we must have captured all its neighbors first. This creates dependency chains.

2. For each cell, we have a choice:
   - Capture directly (pay cost `c[i][j]`)
   - Capture indirectly (pay cost `b[i][j]`, but require all neighbors to be captured)

3. The optimal strategy often involves:
   - Capturing certain cells directly to enable indirect capture of others
   - Not capturing cells directly if we can get the same or better value through indirect capture

4. This is essentially a graph problem where we need to decide which cells to capture directly and which to capture indirectly.

## Strategy

This problem can be solved using dynamic programming with bitmasks or by considering dependencies. However, given the constraints (up to 25×25 = 625 cells), we need a more efficient approach.

The key insight is that for any cell, if we choose to capture it indirectly, all its neighbors must be captured. This means we can think of the grid as having "components" where we capture a group of cells.

A practical approach:
1. For each cell, calculate the net gain if we capture it directly vs indirectly.
2. Use a greedy or DP approach considering dependencies.

Actually, this is a classic problem that can be solved by considering that for any cell, if we capture it indirectly, we must have captured all its neighbors. So we can think of it as a graph where cells are nodes and edges represent dependencies.

Given the complexity, I'll use a simpler approach:
- For each cell, we'll decide whether to capture it directly or indirectly.
- We'll build dependencies and check feasibility.

But after thinking more, this is actually a maximum weight independent set problem with dependencies. 

Let me use a more straightforward approach with DP over subsets for small cases, but since n,m ≤ 25, we need something smarter.

Actually, I think the intended solution is to use the fact that we should capture a cell indirectly only if the benefit outweighs the cost and all neighbors are already captured.

2. However, the capture order matters. We need to process cells in an order where dependencies are satisfied.

3. The optimal strategy might be to capture all cells that have high values and low indirect costs.

- For cells that can be captured indirectly, we need all neighbors captured first.
- We can use a dependency-based approach: process cells in a certain order.

The net gain for a cell is `v[i][j] - cost`, where cost is either `b[i][j]` or `c[i][j]`.

```python
def solve():
```

```python
def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    v = []
    for _ in range(n):
        v.append(list(map(int, input().split())))
    
    b = []
    for _ in range(n):
        b.append(list(map(int, input().split())))
    
    c = []
    for _ in range(n):
        c.append(list(map(int, input().split())))
    
    # For each cell, we can capture it at cost min(b[i][j], c[i][j])
    # But we need to satisfy dependencies for indirect capture
    
    # If a cell has b[i][j] < c[i][j], we might want to capture it indirectly
    # But only if all neighbors are captured
    
    # Create a list of all cells with their information
    cells = []
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(m):
            cells.append((i, j, v[i][j], b[i][j], c[i][j]))
    
    # Sort by value in descending order
    cells.sort(key=lambda x: x[2], reverse=True)
    
    # Keep track of which cells are captured
    captured = [[False] * m for _ in range(n)]
    score = 0
    
    # Process cells in order of highest value
    for i, j, val, cost_b, cost_c in cells:
        # If we can capture this cell indirectly (all neighbors are captured)
        can_capture_indirectly = True
        for di, dj in [(0,1), (0,-1), (1,0), (-1,0)]:
            ni, nj = i + di, j + dj
            if 0 <= ni < n and 0 <= nj < m and not captured[ni][nj]:
                can_capture_indirectly = False
                break
        
        if can_capture_indirectly:
            # Capture indirectly - cost b[i][j]
            cost = cost_b
        else:
            # Capture directly - cost c[i][j]
            cost = cost_c
        
        captured[i][j] = True
        score += val - cost
    
    print(score)

solve()
```

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# but only if dependencies are satisfied

# Process in order of value
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You have an n by m grid. Each grid square has a certain value associated with it. This is given by the numbers vi,j.

You can capture grid squares in two different ways.
You can directly capture a grid square. This costs ci,j.
 You can indirectly capture a grid square. You can only do this if we have already captured all of this squares neighbors. This costs bi,j ≤ ci,j.

Two squares are neighbors if they share an edge.

Your score is the sum of values you have captured minus the cost it took to capture those squares.
Return the maximum possible score you can achieve.

 Input format: 
The first line of input will contain two integers n, m.

The next n lines of input will contain m space separated integers each.
The j-th number on the i-th line denotes vi,j.

The next n lines of input will contain m space separated integers each.
The j-th number on the i-th line denotes bi,j.

The next n lines of input will contain m space separated integers each.
The j-th number on the i-th line denotes ci,j.

 Output format: 
Print a single integer, the answer to the problem.

 Constraints 
For all subtasks:
0 ≤ vi,j ≤ 100,000 
0 ≤ bi,j ≤ ci,j ≤ 100,000 
1 ≤ n, m

 Subtask 1 (45 pts): 
n, m ≤ 3

 Subtask 2 (35 pts): 
n, m ≤ 7

 Subtask 3 (20 pts): 
n, m ≤ 25

0 2 8 6 2

9 1 2 2 0

9 1 8 9 7

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m grid where each cell has:
   - Value `v[i][j]` (what we gain when captured)
   - Indirect cost `b[i][j]` (cost to capture if all neighbors are already captured)
   - Direct cost `c[i][j]` (cost to capture directly)

2. We can capture cells in two ways:
   - Directly: pay cost `c[i][j]`
   - Indirectly: pay cost `b[i][j]` only if all 4 neighbors (up, down, left, right) are already captured

3. Goal: Maximize total value minus total cost.

## Key Insights

1. For any cell, if we capture it indirectly, we must have captured all its neighbors first.

2. The problem has a dependency structure - we can only capture a cell indirectly after all its neighbors are captured.

3. This suggests we should think about which cells to capture directly and which to capture indirectly.

4. For each cell, we have two choices:
   - Capture it directly: cost = c[i][j], gain = v[i][j]
   - Capture it indirectly: cost = b[i][j], gain = v[i][j] (but only if all neighbors are captured)

5. The key insight is that we can use dynamic programming with state representing which cells are captured. However, with n,m ≤ 25, the state space is too large.

## Strategy

Since the constraints are small (up to 25×25), we need a more efficient approach.

Let me think about this differently:

1. For cells that can only be captured indirectly, we must capture all their neighbors first.

2. For a cell to be captured indirectly, we need to have captured all its neighbors.

3. This creates a dependency graph where we need to capture cells in a specific order.

4. The optimal strategy might involve capturing some cells directly and others indirectly.

## Algorithm Approach

This is a classic problem that can be solved using the following insight:

For each cell, we can either:
- Capture it directly (pay c[i][j])
- Capture it indirectly (pay b[i][j]) if all neighbors are captured

The maximum score can be found by trying all possible combinations, but that's exponential.

Alternative approach: use the fact that if we capture a cell indirectly, we're effectively "paying" b[i][j] to get v[i][j]. If we capture it directly, we pay c[i][j] to get v[i][j].

For each cell, we should capture it indirectly only if b[i][j] < c[i][j] and we can satisfy the neighbor constraint.

However, the neighbor constraint makes this complex.

## Greedy with Dependency Resolution

I think the optimal approach is to:
1. First, consider all cells that cannot be captured indirectly (like corner cells or cells with no neighbors).
2. For cells that can be captured indirectly, we need to ensure their neighbors are captured first.

Actually, let me think of this as a graph problem where:
- Cells are nodes
- An edge exists between neighbors
- We want to assign each node to be captured directly or indirectly

But with the dependency constraint, if a cell is captured indirectly, all its neighbors must be captured.

For any cell (i,j):
- If we capture it directly, we pay c[i][j]
- If we capture it indirectly, we pay b[i][j] but only if all neighbors are already captured

Since b[i][j] ≤ c[i][j], the indirect cost is always ≤ direct cost.

So, if we can capture a cell indirectly (all neighbors captured), we should do so as it's cheaper.

But the dependency makes it tricky.

Actually, since b[i][j] ≤ c[i][j], and the goal is to maximize value minus cost, we should always prefer indirect capture when possible.

The algorithm:
1. Create a dependency graph
2. Process cells in topological order (from those with no dependencies to those with many)
3. For each cell, if all neighbors are captured, capture it indirectly (pay b[i][j])
4. Otherwise, capture it directly (pay c[i][j])

But we need to consider that capturing a cell indirectly means we get v[i][j] - b[i][j], while capturing directly gives v[i][j] - c[i][j].

Since b[i][j] ≤ c[i][j], capturing indirectly gives at least as much value.

So the algorithm:
1. Start with no cells captured
2. For each cell in some order, if we can capture it indirectly (all neighbors captured), do so
3. Otherwise, capture it directly

The order matters. We should process cells in an order that ensures dependencies are satisfied.

I'll process cells in row-major order, and for each cell, if it can be captured indirectly, do so; otherwise capture directly.

```python
def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Read values
    v = []
    for _ in range(n):
        row = list(map(int, input().split()))
        v.append(row)
    
    # Read indirect costs
    b = []
    for _ in range(n):
        row = list(map(int, input().split()))
        b.append(row)
    
    # Read direct costs
    c = []
    for _ in range(n):
        row = list(map(int, input().split()))
        c.append(row)
    
    # Track which cells are captured
    captured = [[False] * m for _ in range(n)]
    
    # Track total score
    total_score = 0
    
    # Process each cell in row-major order
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(m):
            # Check if we can capture this cell indirectly
            can_capture_indirectly = True
            
            # Check all four neighbors
            for di, dj in [(0, 1), (0, -1), (1, 0), (-1, 0)]:
                ni, nj = i + di, j + dj
                if 0 <= ni < n and 0 <= nj < m:
                    if not captured[ni][nj]:
                        can_capture_indirectly = False
                        break
            
            if can_capture_indirectly:
                # Capture indirectly - we get value and pay b[i][j]
                total_score += v[i][j] - b[i][j]
                captured[i][j] = True
            else:
                # Can only capture directly
                total_score += v[i][j] - c[i][j]
                captured[i][j] = True
    
    print(total_score)

solve()
```

- For cells that can't be captured indirectly, we capture them directly
- For cells that can be captured indirectly, we capture them indirectly

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Read values
v = []
for _ in range(n):
    row = list(map(int, input().split()))
    v.append(row)

# Read indirect costs
b = []
for _ in range(n):
    row = list(map(int, input().split()))
    b.append(row)

# Read direct costs
c = []
for _ in range(n):
    row = list(map(int, input().split()))
    c.append(row)

# Track which cells are captured
captured = [[False] * m for _ in range(n)]

# Track total score
total_score = 0

# Process each cell in row-major order (i, j)
for i in range(n):
    for j in range(m):
        # Check if we can capture this cell indirectly
        can_capture_indirectly = True
        
        # Check all four neighbors (up, down, left, right)
        for di, dj in [(0, 1), (0, -1), (1, 0), (-1, 0)]:
            ni, nj = i + di, j + dj
            if 0 <= ni < n and 0 <= nj < m:
                # If neighbor exists and not captured, can't capture indirectly
                if not captured[ni][nj]:
                    can_capture_indirectly = False
                    break
        
        if can_capture_indirectly:
            # Capture indirectly - we get value and pay b[i][j]
            total_score += v[i][j] - b[i][j]
            captured[i][j] = True
        else:
            # Capture directly - we get value and pay c[i][j]
            total_score += v[i][j] - c[i][j]
            captured[i][j] = True

print(total_score)
```

                # If neighbor exists and not captured, can't capture indirectly
                if not captured[ni][nj]:
                    can_capture_indirectly = False
                    break
        
        if can_capture_indirectly:
            # Capture indirectly - we get value and pay b[i][j]
            total_score += v[i][j] - b[i][j]
            captured[i][j] = True
        else:
            # Capture directly - we get value and pay c[i][j]
            total_score += v[i][j] - c[i][j]
            captured[i][j] = True

print(total_score)

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You have an n by m grid. Each grid square has a certain value associated with it. This is given by the numbers vi,j.

You can capture grid squares in two different ways.
You can directly capture a grid square. This costs ci,j.
 You can indirectly capture a grid square. You can only do this if we have already captured all of this squares neighbors. This costs bi,j ≤ ci,j.

Two squares are neighbors if they share an edge.

Your score is the sum of values you have captured minus the cost it took to capture those squares.
Return the maximum possible score you can achieve.

 Input format: 
The first line of input will contain two integers n, m.

The next n lines of input will contain m space separated integers each.
The j-th number on the i-th line denotes vi,j.

The j-th number on the i-th line denotes bi,j.

The j-th number on the i-th line denotes ci,j.

 Output format:

0 ≤ bi,j ≤ ci,j ≤ 100,000 
1 ≤ n, m

 Subtask 1 (45 pts): 
n, m ≤ 3

 Subtask 2 (35 pts): 
n, m ≤ 7

n, m ≤ 25

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m grid where each cell has:
   - A value `v[i][j]` (what we gain from capturing it)
   - A base cost `b[i][j]` (cost to indirectly capture)
   - A direct cost `c[i][j]` (cost to directly capture)

2. We can capture a square in two ways:
   - Directly: cost is `c[i][j]`
   - Indirectly: only if ALL NEIGHBORS are already captured, cost is `b[i][j]` (and `b[i][j] ≤ c[i][j]`)

3. Goal: Maximize (sum of values of captured squares) - (sum of costs to capture those squares)

## Key Insights

1. If we capture a cell indirectly, we must have captured all its neighbors first.
2. We can think of this as a dependency problem where some cells must be captured before others.
3. For each cell, we have two choices:
   - Capture it directly (pay c[i][j], get v[i][j])
   - Capture it indirectly (pay b[i][j], get v[i][j]) - but only if all neighbors are captured

4. Since indirect capture costs less than direct capture, we generally prefer indirect capture when possible.

5. However, we need to consider the dependencies. If a cell's neighbors are captured indirectly, then we can capture this cell indirectly.

1. Start with cells that have no unmet neighbor dependencies

3. Since b[i][j] ≤ c[i][j], capturing indirectly always saves cost unless we have to pay for dependencies

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You have an n by m grid. Each grid square has a certain value associated with it. This is given by the numbers vi,j.

You can capture grid squares in two different ways.
You can directly capture a grid square. This costs ci,j.
 You can indirectly capture a grid square. You can only do this if we have already captured all of this squares neighbors. This costs bi,j ≤ ci,j.

Two squares are neighbors if they share an edge.

Your score is the sum of values you have captured minus the cost it took to capture those squares.
Return the maximum possible score you can achieve.

 Input format: 
The first line of input will contain two integers n, m.

The next n lines of input will contain m space separated integers each.
The j-th number on the i-th line denotes vi,j.

The next n lines of input will contain m space separated integers each.
The j-th number on the i-th line denotes bi,j.

The next n lines of input will contain m space separated integers each.
The j-th number on the i-th line denotes ci,j.

 Output format: 
Print a single integer, the answer to the problem.

 Constraints 
For all subtasks:
0 ≤ vi,j ≤ 100,000 
0 ≤ bi,j ≤ ci,j ≤ 100,000 
1 ≤ n, m

 Subtask 1 (45 pts): 
n, m ≤ 3

 Subtask 2 (35 pts): 
n, m ≤ 7

 Subtask 3 (20 pts): 
n, m ≤ 25

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m grid with values `v[i][j]` for each cell
2. We can capture cells in two ways:
   - Directly: cost `c[i][j]`, get value `v[i][j]`
   - Indirectly: cost `b[i][j]` (where `b[i][j] ≤ c[i][j]`), but only if ALL neighbors are already captured
3. Our score = sum of captured values - sum of capture costs
4. We want to maximize the score

## Key Insights

1. For indirect capture, we need all neighbors to be captured first. This creates a dependency structure.
2. If we capture a cell indirectly, we get value `v[i][j]` but pay cost `b[i][j]`.
3. If we capture a cell directly, we get value `v[i][j]` but pay cost `c[i][j]`.
4. The optimal choice for each cell depends on whether we capture it directly or indirectly.

## Strategy Analysis

For each cell, we have two choices:
- Capture directly: cost `c[i][j]`
- Capture indirectly: cost `b[i][j]` (only if all neighbors are captured)

Since indirect capture costs less than direct capture (`b[i][j] ≤ c[i][j]`), we generally want to use indirect capture when possible.

However, for indirect capture to work, all neighbors must be captured. This creates a dependency chain.

## Algorithm Approach

This is essentially a graph problem where:
- Each cell is a node
- We can capture a node indirectly only after all its neighbors are captured
- We need to decide the optimal order of capture

The key insight is that we should consider the net gain for each cell:
- Net gain if captured directly: `v[i][j] - c[i][j]`
- Net gain if captured indirectly: `v[i][j] - b[i][j]`

But indirect capture requires all neighbors to be captured first.

I think the optimal approach is to use a greedy strategy with BFS/DFS, starting from cells that can be captured indirectly or directly.

Wait, actually we need to think about the dependency graph more carefully.

Let me try a different approach:

The optimal strategy seems to be:
- For each cell, we get value `v[i][j]` regardless of how we capture it
- The cost depends on the method we choose
- We want to minimize total cost while capturing all cells

Since we must capture all cells (otherwise we can't get their values), the problem becomes: assign each cell to either direct or indirect capture to minimize total cost.

But indirect capture requires that all neighbors are captured (either directly or indirectly).

This creates a complex dependency.

Actually, let me think of it this way: if we capture a cell indirectly, all its neighbors must be captured first. So the capture order matters.

I think the solution is to use a greedy algorithm that processes cells in a certain order, always preferring indirect capture when possible.

But let me implement a simpler approach - since the constraints are small, we might try a different perspective.

Actually, I think the key insight is that we can capture any cell indirectly if all its neighbors are already captured. So we can think of this as a process where we build up the capture set.

We should start with the cells that have the highest "net benefit" from indirect capture.

But let me implement a BFS-like approach where we start with cells that can be captured indirectly, then expand.

Final approach:
1. For each cell, calculate the net gain if captured indirectly vs directly
2. Use a priority queue to process cells that can be captured indirectly first
3. Always choose indirect capture when possible (since it's cheaper)

```python
from collections import deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Read values
    v = []
    for _ in range(n):
        row = list(map(int, input().split()))
        v.append(row)
    
    # Read indirect costs
    b = []
    for _ in range(n):
        row = list(map(int, input().split()))
        b.append(row)
    
    # Read direct costs
    c = []
    for _ in range(n):
        row = list(map(int, input().split()))
        c.append(row)
    
    # For each cell, calculate the net gain
    # If captured directly: v[i][j] - c[i][j]
    # If captured indirectly: v[i][j] - b[i][j]
    # We prefer indirect capture when possible
    
    # We'll use BFS to simulate capture process
    # Start with cells that can be captured indirectly (all neighbors already captured)
    
    # Create a visited array to track which cells are captured
    captured = [[False] * m for _ in range(n)]
    
    # Create a queue of cells that can be captured next
    # Initially, no cell has all neighbors captured, so we can't start with indirect capture
    # Instead, we need to capture some cells directly first
    
    # Actually, we can capture any cell directly at cost c[i][j]
    # Or capture a cell indirectly at cost b[i][j] if all neighbors are captured
    
    # First, let's consider that we can choose any order
    # We want to minimize total cost
    
    # Alternative approach: try to capture as many cells as possible indirectly
    # We need to find a valid sequence of captures
    
    # Since the constraints are small, let's try a different insight:
    # The total value we get is sum of all v[i][j]
    # The total cost is sum of either b[i][j] or c[i][j] for each cell
    # We want to minimize cost, so we prefer b[i][j] over c[i][j] when possible
    
    # But "when possible" means all neighbors are captured
    
    # This is equivalent to: we can capture cell (i,j) indirectly if all its neighbors are captured
    # So we need to build up the capture set
    
    # We can use a greedy approach: at each step, if there's any cell whose neighbors are all captured,
    # we can capture it indirectly (at cost b[i][j]), otherwise we capture a cell directly (at cost c[i][j])
    
    # But which cell to capture directly? We should choose one that minimizes cost or maximizes value
    
    # Actually, we need to capture all cells, and we want to minimize total cost.
    
    # Let's use a priority queue approach:
    # Initially, no cell can be captured indirectly (no neighbors captured)
    # We need to capture some cells directly first.
    
    # We can capture any cell directly at cost c[i][j]
    # Or capture a cell indirectly at cost b[i][j] if all neighbors are captured
    
    # Algorithm:
    # 1. Keep track of which cells are captured
    # 2. Initially, no cells are captured
    # 3. While there are un-captured cells:
    #    a. If there exists a cell where all neighbors are captured, capture it indirectly (cost b[i][j])
    #    b. Otherwise, capture a cell directly (choose the one with minimum cost, or maximum value minus cost?)
    
    # But we want to maximize value minus cost, so for direct capture, we want to minimize cost relative to value.
    
    # Actually, since we get value v[i][j] regardless, we want to minimize cost.
    # So for direct capture, we should choose the cell with minimum c[i][j] among un-captured cells.
    
    # Let's implement this greedy algorithm:
    
    captured = [[False] * m for _ in range(n)]
    total_cost = 0
    total_value = 0
    
    # We'll use a set to keep track of cells that can be captured indirectly
    # Initially, no cells can be captured indirectly
    
    queue = deque()
    
    # First, let's initialize the queue with cells that might be captureable
    # Actually, we need to start with some captures
    
    # Instead, let's use the fact that we need to capture all cells
    # We'll iterate until all cells are captured
    
    # Add all cells to a priority queue with their direct capture cost
    import heapq
    
    pq = []
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(m):
            # Cell (i,j) can be captured directly at cost c[i][j]
            heapq.heappush(pq, (c[i][j], i, j))
    
    # Also, we can capture indirectly if neighbors are captured
    # But initially, no neighbors are captured, so no indirect captures yet
    
    # We'll maintain a captured set
    captured = [[False] * m for _ in range(n)]
    
    # Process cells until all are captured
    while pq:
        cost, i, j = heapq.heappop(pq)
        
        # If already captured, skip
        if captured[i][j]:
            continue

        # All neighbors must already be captured

            if 0 <= ni < n and 0 <= nj < m:

                    can_indirect = False
                    break

            # Capture indirectly - it's cheaper
            total_cost += b[i][j]
            captured[i][j] = True
            # Add this cell to the queue to check if it enables indirect captures of others
            # But we don't need to add it back since we're just removing from pq
        else:

        # and add those to the priority queue with indirect cost

        break

    # and we want to minimize cost, the optimal strategy is:

    # - Otherwise, we capture it directly at cost c[i][j]

    total_cost = 0
    pq = []  # priority queue of (cost, i, j) for direct captures

    # Add all cells to priority queue with direct capture cost
```

```python
import heapq

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Read values
    v = []
    for _ in range(n):
        row = list(map(int, input().split()))
        v.append(row)
    
    # Read indirect costs
    b = []
    for _ in range(n):
        row = list(map(int, input().split()))
        b.append(row)
    
    # Read direct costs
    c = []
    for _ in range(n):
        row = list(map(int, input().split()))
        c.append(row)
    
    # We'll simulate the capture process
    captured = [[False] * m for _ in range(n)]
    
    # Use a priority queue to process cells
    # Initially, all cells can be captured directly at cost c[i][j]
    pq = []
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(m):
            heapq.heappush(pq, (c[i][j], i, j))
    
    total_cost = 0
    
    while pq:
        cost, i, j = heapq.heappop(pq)
        
        if captured[i][j]:
            continue
            
        # Check if we can capture this cell indirectly (all neighbors are captured)
        can_indirect = True
        for di, dj in [(0,1), (0,-1), (1,0), (-1,0)]:
            ni, nj = i + di, j + dj
            if 0 <= ni < n and 0 <= nj < m:
                if not captured[ni][nj]:
                    can_indirect = False
                    break
        
        if can_indirect:
            # Capture indirectly - it's cheaper
            total_cost += b[i][j]
            captured[i][j] = True
        else:
            # Capture directly
            total_cost += c[i][j]
            captured[i][j] = True
        
        # After capturing (i,j), check if any other un-captured cell now has all neighbors captured
        # and add them to the pq with their indirect cost
        # This ensures we consider indirect capture opportunities
        for x in range(n):
            for y in range(m):
                if captured[x][y]:
                    continue
                # Check if all neighbors of (x,y) are captured
                can_be_indirect = True
                for dx, dy in [(0,1), (0,-1), (1,0), (-1,0)]:
                    nx, ny = x + dx, y + dy
                    if 0 <= nx < n and 0 <= ny < m:
                        if not captured[nx][ny]:
                            can_be_indirect = False
                            break
                if can_be_indirect:
                    # This cell can be captured indirectly at cost b[x][y]
                    heapq.heappush(pq, (b[x][y], x, y))
    
    # Calculate total value
    total_value = sum(sum(row) for row in v)
    
    # Score is total value minus total cost
    score = total_value - total_cost
    print(score)

solve()
```

This should work. The algorithm:

1. Starts with a priority queue containing all cells with their direct capture cost
2. At each step, pops the cell with minimum cost
3. If all neighbors are already captured, captures it indirectly at cost b[i][j]
4. Otherwise, captures it directly at cost c[i][j]
5. After each capture, checks if any other un-captured cell now has all neighbors captured, and adds such cells to the queue with their indirect cost
6. Finally, returns (total value - total cost)

This ensures we always make the cheapest possible capture at each step, prioritizing indirect capture when possible.

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You have an n by m grid. Each grid square has a certain value associated with it. This is given by the numbers vi,j.

You can capture grid squares in two different ways.
You can directly capture a grid square. This costs ci,j.
 You can indirectly capture a grid square. You can only do this if we have already captured all of this squares neighbors. This costs bi,j ≤ ci,j.

Two squares are neighbors if they share an edge.

Your score is the sum of values you have captured minus the cost it took to capture those squares.
Return the maximum possible score you can achieve.

 Input format: 
The first line of input will contain two integers n, m.

The next n lines of input will contain m space separated integers each.
The j-th number on the i-th line denotes vi,j.

The next n lines of input will contain m space separated integers each.
The j-th number on the i-th line denotes bi,j.

The next n lines of input will contain m space separated integers each.
The j-th number on the i-th line denotes ci,j.

 Output format: 
Print a single integer, the answer to the problem.

 Constraints 
For all subtasks:
0 ≤ vi,j ≤ 100,000 
0 ≤ bi,j ≤ ci,j ≤ 100,000 
1 ≤ n, m

 Subtask 1 (45 pts): 
n, m ≤ 3

 Subtask 2 (35 pts): 
n, m ≤ 7

 Subtask 3 (20 pts): 
n, m ≤ 25

1. We have an n×m grid with each cell having a value `v[i][j]`
2. We can capture cells in two ways:
   - **Directly**: Cost `c[i][j]`, captures the square directly
   - **Indirectly**: Cost `b[i][j] ≤ c[i][j]`, can only do this if ALL neighbors are already captured
3. Score = sum of values of captured squares - total cost
4. Goal: Maximize the score

4. However, capturing a cell indirectly requires all its neighbors to be captured, which creates dependencies.

```python
def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Read values
    v = []
    for _ in range(n):
        v.append(list(map(int, input().split())))
    
    # Read indirect costs
    b = []
    for _ in range(n):
        b.append(list(map(int, input().split())))
    
    # Read direct costs
    c = []
    for _ in range(n):
        c.append(list(map(int, input().split())))
    
    # Track captured status
    captured = [[False] * m for _ in range(n)]
    
    # Queue for cells that can be captured indirectly
    from collections import deque
    queue = deque()
    
    # Initially, no cells can be captured indirectly
    # But we need to add cells that might become eligible
    
    # Instead, let's do this: any cell that has all neighbors captured can be captured indirectly
    # We'll iterate until no more changes happen
    
    while True:
        changed = False
        for i in range(n):
            for j in range(m):
                # Check if this cell can be captured indirectly
                # All neighbors must be captured
                can_capture = True
                # Check all 4 neighbors (up, down, left, right)
                for di, dj in [(-1, 0), (1, 0), (0, -1), (0, 1)]: 
                    ni, nj = i + di, j + dj
                    if 0 <= ni < n and 0 <= nj < m:
                        if not captured[ni][nj]:
                            can_capture = False
                            break
                
                if can_capture and not captured[i][j]:
                    # Capture this cell indirectly
                    captured[i][j] = True
                    changed = True
                    # We might now be able to capture other cells indirectly
        if not changed:
            break
    
    # Now, any cells not captured must be captured directly
    total_value = 0
    total_cost = 0
    
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(m):
            if captured[i][j]:
                total_value += v[i][j]
                # If captured indirectly, cost is b[i][j]
                # If captured directly, cost is c[i][j]
                # But in our algorithm, captured cells are captured indirectly
                # unless we need to capture them directly for some reason
                # Actually, we only capture indirectly when we can
                # So captured cells are captured indirectly
                # But this isn't right - we need to consider that cells might be captured directly
                
    # Wait, I see a flaw. In my algorithm, I'm only capturing cells that have all neighbors captured.
    # But what about cells that we capture directly? I haven't added that part.
    
    # Let me restart. The algorithm should be:
    # 1. Start with no cells captured.
    # 2. While there exists a cell that has all neighbors captured, capture it indirectly.
    # 3. After this process, any cells not captured are captured directly.
    
    # But the order matters for when we capture first.
    
    # Actually, I think my algorithm above is correct in spirit, but I need to add the direct capture part.
    
    # After the indirect capture loop, any cell not captured gets captured directly.
    
    total_value = 0
    total_cost = 0
    
    # First, add value from all captured cells
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(m):
            total_value += v[i][j]
    
    # Now add costs
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(m):
            if captured[i][j]:
                # This cell was captured indirectly, so cost is b[i][j]
                total_cost += b[i][j]
            else:
                # This cell was captured directly, cost is c[i][j]
                total_cost += c[i][j]
    
    return total_value - total_cost

print(solve())
```

Wait, I think there's a flaw. In the above algorithm, we might not capture all cells that could have been captured indirectly. Also, we're capturing a cell indirectly only when all neighbors are already captured, which might not give us the optimal solution.

Let me reconsider the problem. Maybe the optimal solution is to capture some cells directly and then use the indirect capture for others.

- Capturing a cell indirectly always saves at least (c[i][j] - b[i][j]) cost, which is non-negative.
- We want to maximize savings, so we should capture any cell that can be captured indirectly whenever possible.
- The order of processing doesn't matter because if a cell can be captured indirectly, it will eventually be captured, and capturing it indirectly doesn't prevent other cells from being captured indirectly.

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Read values
v = []
for _ in range(n):
    v.append(list(map(int, input().split())))

# Read indirect costs
b = []
for _ in range(n):
    b.append(list(map(int, input().split())))

# Read direct costs
c = []
for _ in range(n):
    c.append(list(map(int, input().split())))

# Track captured status
captured = [[False] * m for _ in range(n)]

# Queue for cells that can be captured indirectly
from collections import deque
queue = deque()

# We'll use a while loop to keep processing until no more indirect captures are possible
changed = True
while changed:
    changed = False
    # Check all cells
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(m):
            # Check if all neighbors are captured
            can_capture = True
            for di, dj in [(-1, 0), (1, 0), (0, -1), (0, 1)]:
                ni, nj = i + di, j + dj
                if 0 <= ni < n and 0 <= nj < m and not captured[ni][nj]:
                    can_capture = False
                    break
            
            if can_capture and not captured[i][j]:
                # Capture this cell indirectly
                captured[i][j] = True
                changed = True

# Now calculate total score
total_value = sum(sum(row) for row in v)
total_cost = 0

for i in range(n):
    for j in range(m):
        if captured[i][j]:
            total_cost += b[i][j]
        else:
            total_cost += c[i][j]

print(total_value - total_cost)
```

I believe this should work. The algorithm:
1. Repeatedly finds cells that have all neighbors captured and captures them indirectly.
2. After completion, any remaining cells are captured directly.
3. The score is calculated as total value minus total cost.

This maximizes savings from indirect capture whenever possible.

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You have an n by m grid. Each grid square has a certain value associated with it. This is given by the numbers vi,j.

You can capture grid squares in two different ways.
You can directly capture a grid square. This costs ci,j.
 You can indirectly capture a grid square. You can only do this if we have already captured all of this squares neighbors. This costs bi,j ≤ ci,j.

Two squares are neighbors if they share an edge.

Your score is the sum of values you have captured minus the cost it took to capture those squares.
Return the maximum possible score you can achieve.

 Input format: 
The first line of input will contain two integers n, m.

The next n lines of input will contain m space separated integers each.
The j-th number on the i-th line denotes vi,j.

The next n lines of input will contain m space separated integers each.
The j-th number on the i-th line denotes bi,j.

The next n lines of input will contain m space separated integers each.
The j-th number on the i-th line denotes ci,j.

 Output format: 
Print a single integer, the answer to the problem.

 Constraints 
For all subtasks:
0 ≤ vi,j ≤ 100,000 
0 ≤ bi,j ≤ ci,j ≤ 100,000 
1 ≤ n, m

 Subtask 1 (45 pts): 
n, m ≤ 3

 Subtask 2 (35 pts): 
n, m ≤ 7

 Subtask 3 (20 pts): 
n, m ≤ 25

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m grid where each cell has:
   - Value `v[i][j]` (positive contribution to score)
   - Indirect capture cost `b[i][j]` (can capture if all neighbors are captured)
   - Direct capture cost `c[i][j]` (can capture directly)

2. We can capture cells in two ways:
   - Directly: pay cost `c[i][j]`
   - Indirectly: pay cost `b[i][j]` only if all 4 orthogonal neighbors are already captured

3. Score = sum of values of captured cells - total cost of capturing those cells

4. Goal: maximize the score.

## Key Insights

1. For indirect capture to work, we need all neighbors to be captured first. This creates a dependency.

2. We can think of this as a graph problem where each cell is a node, and we need to determine optimal capture order.

3. The key insight is that if we capture a cell indirectly, we get its value but pay `b[i][j]`. If we capture it directly, we get its value but pay `c[i][j]`.

4. For any cell, if we can capture it indirectly, we should do so only if `b[i][j] < c[i][j]` and all neighbors are captured.

5. However, we need to consider the dependency - capturing a cell might enable indirect capture of others.

## Strategy

This is actually a max flow/min cut problem or can be solved with dynamic programming with state space of captured cells. However, given the constraints (n, m ≤ 25), we need a more efficient approach.

```python
import heapq

n, m = map(int, input().split())

v = []
for _ in range(n):
    v.append(list(map(int, input().split())))

b = []
for _ in range(n):
    b.append(list(map(int, input().split())))

c = []
for _ in range(n):
    c.append(list(map(int, input().split())))

# captured[i][j] = True if cell (i,j) is captured
captured = [[False] * m for _ in range(n)]

# Create a priority queue of cells, ordered by benefit of indirect capture (v[i][j] - b[i][j]) descending
# Higher benefit first
pq = []
for i in range(n):
    for j in range(m):
        if v[i][j] - b[i][j] > 0:  # Only consider if indirect capture gives benefit
            heapq.heappush(pq, (- (v[i][j] - b[i][j]), i, j))

# Process cells in order of benefit
while pq:
    neg_benefit, i, j = heapq.heappop(pq)
    
    # Check if we can capture this cell indirectly (all neighbors are captured)
    can_indirect = True
    for di, dj in [(0,1), (0,-1), (1,0), (-1,0)]:
        ni, nj = i + di, j + dj
        if 0 <= ni < n and 0 <= nj < m and not captured[ni][nj]:
            can_indirect = False
            break
    
    if can_indirect:
        # Capture indirectly
        captured[i][j] = True
        # No need to add to score here - we'll calculate at end
    else:
        # Cannot capture indirectly, capture directly
        captured[i][j] = True

# Calculate total score
total_score = 0
for i in range(n):
    for j in range(m):
        if captured[i][j]:
            if captured[i][j]:  # We captured it
                # If captured indirectly, cost is b[i][j]
                # If captured directly, cost is c[i][j]
                # But we don't know how it was captured
                # Actually, we need to know the capture method
                
                # In our algorithm, if we captured indirectly, we know it's because all neighbors were captured
                # But we don't track it
                
                # We need to modify: track the capture method
                
                pass

# This approach is flawed because we don't know if it was captured indirectly or directly.
```

I need to track the capture method. Let me track:

- captured[i][j] = True if captured
- method[i][j] = 0 for indirect, 1 for direct

```python
import heapq

n, m = map(int, input().split())

v = []
for _ in range(n):
    v.append(list(map(int, input().split())))

b = []
for _ in range(n):
    b.append(list(map(int, input().split())))

c = []
for _ in range(n):
    c.append(list(map(int, input().split())))

# captured[i][j] = True if cell (i,j) is captured
captured = [[False] * m for _ in range(n)]
# method[i][j] = 0 for indirect, 1 for direct
method = [[-1] * m for _ in range(n)]

# Create a priority queue of cells, ordered by benefit of indirect capture (v[i][j] - b[i][j]) descending
pq = []
for i in range(n):
    for j in range(m):
        # Only consider if indirect capture is beneficial
        if v[i][j] - b[i][j] > v[i][j] - c[i][j]:  # indirect is better
            heapq.heappush(pq, (- (v[i][j] - b[i][j]), i, j))

# Process cells in order of benefit
while pq:
    neg_benefit, i, j = heapq.heappop(pq)
    
    # Check if we can capture this cell indirectly (all neighbors are captured)
    can_indirect = True
    for di, dj in [(0,1), (0,-1), (1,0), (-1,0)]:
        ni, nj = i + di, j + dj
        if 0 <= ni < n and 0 <= nj < m and not captured[ni][nj]:
            can_indirect = False
            break
    
    if can_indirect:
        # Capture indirectly
        captured[i][j] = True
        method[i][j] = 0
    else:
        # Capture directly
        captured[i][j] = True
        method[i][j] = 1

# Calculate total score
total_score = 0
for i in range(n):
    for j in range(m):
        if captured[i][j]:
            if method[i][j] == 0:  # indirect capture
                total_score += v[i][j] - b[i][j]
            else:  # direct capture
                total_score += v[i][j] - c[i][j]

print(total_score)
```
